ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e130	The Presidency is presenting today its position on the regulation of remuneration to the representatives of the big business and the members of the government. The Presidency promotes a unanimous proposal that the Stortinget set out the remuneration proposed by the Enlarged Bureau in its position. The Commission has obtained information from several teams to monitor wage developments in Norwegian working life, from both private and public activities. The Commission also refers to the agreement on the level of settlement of the year. As in previous years, the Wage and Occupational Pensions Commission has focused on the expected annual wage growth at year-to-year settlement. The position of the Wage and Wage Commission represents an annual wage increase in 2015 for the gross domestic product representatives and the government’s 2,7 p.m. members, on the basis of calculated annual wage growth in both the state, municipal and private sectors. The Presidency indicates that the Wage and Wage Commission has looked at the wage developments of gross domestic product representatives and government members in relation to general wage developments as well as central government leadership groups in the period 1999–2013. To this end, the Wage and Employment Commission comments on the fact that in the development of wages and salaries between the above-mentioned management groups and grossing-up representatives and government members, a distance has developed over time. The Presidency notes that the Commission in its settlement of the year does not consider it appropriate to make changes to the method of calculation of remuneration, and furthermore that the Commission in its forthcoming salary settlement will come back to the way in which the distance of wage developments is to be dealt with. On this basis, the recommendation of the Presidency is recommended.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e200	Let me start the debate on the police of the future by stating that we in Norway have a very competent and motivated police force. The day around the year, they do their utmost to create safe communities. This debate and the whole reform of the police force are about making the police even better able to solve their social mission. Over several years, it has become clearer that a reform of the police is needed. Crime trends are facing new challenges for the police. The police analysis is clear on this, and it was stated in the report of the Gjørv Commission: ‘The ability of the authorities to protect the human beings in the Outlying Sea failed. A faster police action was really possible. The perpetrator could have stopped earlier 22/7.» This reinforces the need for reform and provides a gloomy backbone for reform. So preparedness, culture and leadership are very important issues in this debate. In addition, experience up to these days has shown that research is a subject that also needs to be raised more clearly. In March the government presented its proposal for the development of the Norwegian police, based on an agreement between the government parties and the left. The proposition of the near-police reform paves the way for the police of the future. It lays the foundation for good police services throughout the country. Action by the neighbourhood reform in both quality and structure will shape the Norwegian police in the next 20–30 years. Today’s organisation in 27 police districts has resulted in far too many differences between police districts and has made it difficult to develop strong enough professional environments in the smaller and medium-sized police districts. Close police reform addresses this and reduces the number of police districts from 27 to 12. With 12 police districts, larger and more robust professional environments are created to investigate and prevent crime, and there are good conditions for having specialist environments that can be developed and maintained over time in each police district, while improving and strengthening the coordination of police response resources. The co-location of police operation centres and fire brigade emergency services does the same. Today’s police district structure has provided accidental centralisation. As a result, the large police districts and the national special agencies of the police have been allocated relatively much of the police’s overall resources. The structural changes in the near-police reform address these challenges and allocate policy relevance and capacity more evenly across the country. The close police reform strengthens the closeness and presence of the police in which the population lives and facilitates a police service that meets the expectations of professional competence in prevention, investigation, preparedness and availability. The organisation of the police shall be organised in such a way as to enable the public to feel that it has an accessible police force when the police services are needed. Availability and preparedness are not ensured by maintaining a short-term, low-staffing labour office, but with more police officers competent to deal with the situations that may arise. The objective of the close police reform is to establish a close police force, operational, visible and accessible, with the capacity and competence to prevent, investigate and prosecute criminal acts, and to ensure the security of citizens. It shall have local anchoring, close contact with the surrounding environment and good cooperation between actors such as non-governmental organisations, municipalities and business. The report from the Gjørv Commission stresses that culture, attitudes and leadership were crucial for the good parts of police management, and those who did not go well. That is why the near-police reform promises leadership, culture and policing attitudes. A trust-based approach has been chosen. Good management and culture is not something that can be adopted politically or something that comes into place overnight. Police management must be based on professional freedom, trust in employees and clear responsibilities. For the Right, it has been important to give the police both confidence and space for action when they are about to begin the extensive conversion work that will lead to the police of the future. An appropriate structure and organisation is an important prerequisite for good governance. With 12 police districts and delegation of decision-making powers to the police, conditions are set up to enable the police director and the country’s police officers to exercise effective strategic direction of the country’s police. But at the same time, let me stress very clearly that the police manage the monopoly of civil society for the exercise of power. This exercise of power must be governed by clear political requirements, decisions, limitations and control mechanisms in the form of laws, regulations and budgets. Strengthening management training in the police, recruitment from outside and management training from other institutions are some of the measures that will help ensure the provision of new impulses to managers and better anchor management in the establishment. Experience learning shall be strengthened and systematised. I would like to thank the parties in the Committee on Legal Affairs and Citizens' Rights for their cooperation. The government's proposal would be given to the majority in the Grand Duchy by the Left. However, it was important for the government parties and the Left to facilitate a broad political settlement when we are about to launch a course for the future police. It is the responsibility of a common political responsibility to ensure the necessary stability and predictability of the police. It enhances the security of the community, improves the security of society and enables the police to develop better services for citizens. The broad agreement with the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party is therefore very important, and it means much more than the mathematical sum of the number of large-scale representatives who are voting for the near-police reform today. I will commend the constructive and solution-oriented approach of these parties. I would also like to highlight the good and honest discussions we have had with the Socialist Left Party, the Centre Party and the Greens. We agree a lot, even if they don't participate in the deal. The agreement continues the main priorities and the main lines of the proposition. It reinforces and clarifies what is proposed on the research side and gives clear political signals of increased quality. The prosecution authority is also very important in this picture. That is why a prosecution analysis must be carried out. The settlement clarifies local participation in the change process of service locations. The decision-making authority is with the police, but the municipalities concerned are more closely involved in the process. Local democratic considerations are addressed through clear demands for local anchoring and through political handling of complaints from municipalities that disagree with the decision of the police. The agreement underlines the importance of crime prevention and the importance of halting the development of crime at an early stage. A well-functioning interface between police and community is a prerequisite for good prevention and crime prevention. The agreement has resulted in some adaptations to the structure of the 12 police districts. The exposure has been strong and positive in several places in the country to influence the borderline between districts. Both the proposition and the conciliation have assessed and balanced these on the basis of police advice and local wishes. The majority today gives a mission to the Ministry of Justice, and therefore also to the police – a mission that is becoming very demanding and very important. Police shall now initiate organisational processes, professional development processes and management development processes to an extent that is lacking in the history of Norwegian police. The size has great expectations, and we make strong demands. We do that in confidence in the professional integrity, the strong social commitment and the common understanding of the social mission of the establishment. I wish everyone involved the best of luck. Finally – to the vote: the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left will vote in favour of proposal 1, from the Labour Party and the Centre, on the liability of the socket.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e383	Today, we are dealing with a reform that is probably one of the most important reforms – and issues – that we are going to deal with during this major period. The backcloth of the matter today is in many ways serious. At the same time, this is a reform that has been delayed, not least by those who have their daily activities in the police and the prosecution authority. Some cases have shown that the Norwegian police have faced management and structural challenges. Recently, we have been confirmed by the tragic Monika case in Bergen, where the investigation of the murder of an 8 year-old girl was extremely deficient. 22 July showed that governance and coordination were failing as needed most. The Gjørv Commission’s report therefore highlighted culture and leadership as the main challenge in the Norwegian police. Let me make a point: there is a lot of good work done in the Norwegian police department. As a lawyer, I have had the pleasure of visiting and meeting many police officers and women, and with self-esteem, I have seen the job done. You just get proud. However, changes are needed, and police reform sets out clear tools to remedy the current situation: Better management training and innovation in recruitment are just some of the measures. The people deserve security through a good police force, and to get a good police force, we have to have good leaders. Clear demands for police time lead to a change in the location of police officers' offices. It's just 4 pst today. by the daily staff offices. This is not sustainable, the public needs to be able to contact the police even outside the ordinary office hours. Therefore, accessibility is now required. An accessible police force is absolutely necessary, and it is therefore also gratifying to work to introduce good IT solutions such as: will enable the delivery of notifications over the Internet. The reform implies 90 p.m. the population shall have a maximum of 45 minutes’ drive to the nearest police office. This requirement is not applicable on a national basis, but in each police district. This will lead to the setting up of a number of offices, but it will also lead to the creation of new offices, or mobile units, somewhere else. The creation of new offices will be relevant where distances are large. The goal is for our people all over the country to have the same good police service. That is why we call this reform the close police reform. The Progress Party wants to bind as few policemen as possible to sit in the office and want as many policemen as possible in the streets. It is also important that the police are not only present in office hours; in the evening most of the crime takes place. The Progress Party wants the police to be more present where crime is taking place, and to be much more visible in the city and built than is the case in many places today. Through the budgets we have now committed – the last two budgets – we have put in place 725 new police posts in the last two years. What we do through the budget, combined with what we now do through the police reform, will, of course, give a more visible – and better – police force. Several of the tasks that the police have solved today will now be solved by others. The progress party is pleased that we are now getting cleaned up in the police task portfolio. A number of tasks that are not really police tasks will now be able to be transferred to other agencies and actors. The police reform also makes it clear that 95 p.m. of the calls to the emergency number shall be answered within 20 seconds. Emergency centres shall no longer be staffed at night, when most of the crime is, after all, committed. There should be police officers on the job all day, so the reform will now lead to a significant increase in minimum staffing. If you call the police in an emergency, you should now receive a reply within a reasonable time – point. One of today's police problems has been that in small police districts, there has been no possibility of sufficient competence in the investigation of serious crimes such as murder, morality and violence. The districts have been too small to be able to employ enough personnel in specialty research positions, and there has been too little training. Then it's very happy that we now get bigger and more powerful districts. I look forward to the implementation of the reform. The fact that we have such a wide-ranging deal in the United Kingdom today is an important premise for the success of this reform. Finally, let me congratulate those with whom we cooperate in this matter, and commend the case rapporteur, who has shriveled us through both negotiations and this matter in the committee. Let me wish the country's police officers and women happiness with the implementation of the reform!

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e461	The police have been watching for a long time. The budget has been subject to a lot of criticism and has even wanted to change for a long time. The focus has sometimes been one-sided negative. So today, when we are dealing with new police reform, I have to say thank you to all the policemen and women who are doing heroic work. Usually we'll hear about the times that go wrong, and not the thousands of episodes that go well. Where things go well, it's often thanks to a police officer who has high confidence in the population, and who's there when people need it. When we are about to adopt new police reform and make the major changes, it has been essential to address what has gone wrong. Without drying to do so, we cannot bring about changes that will improve the Norwegian police force. The police have been the subject of many analyses and reports that have revealed several improvement points. The July 22 report was a thorough review of the state of the Norwegian police force and has been – and will remain – an important guide to ensure that the police are able to carry out their mission. The police themselves have put in place a lot of measures to rectify several situations. That's good. But we politicians must also take our responsibilities. I am therefore very pleased that the government parties and the Left wanted to negotiate with the rest of the Stortinget – and that they were willing to extend, so that we got a broad deal with the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party. It is important to ensure the legitimacy and predictability of the reform, not only for the police but also for the citizens of our country. I would particularly like to thank the case rapporteur for a good job. In the work on new police reform, which has been well over a year for our part, the Christian People's Party has always been committed to ensuring a police force that is present where people live – not because we believe security lies in an office and signs on the wall, but because we believe it is vital to have the police present to strengthen the prevention and thus the fight against crime. In the process of creating the police of the future, we have been committed to enabling the police to investigate better and solve more crime – a police force capable of dealing with the great terrorist attacks, but also a police force capable of dealing with the terrorist attacks in the lives of every single person, if one experiences a killing, a rape, or an invasion of his house, and a police force in which both we politicians and police take lessons from the report of the Gjørv Commission went wrong. They said among other things:\t\t ‘Trading 22/7 identifies the need for many types of change: in planning and regulation, in the disposal of competences and resources, in organisational culture, priorities and focus, yes, even in society’s attitudes. Some of these amendments may be adopted by an authority. It is the simplest changes to make, if only the political will is there. Other and more fundamental changes – in attitudes, leadership and culture – need to be developed over time.’ There are these changes, and not least the prerequisites for these changes to happen, which we are going to adopt today. At the same time, we know that reform work is incredibly demanding, and it may take a long time before we see the results. The police are faced with a demanding task, and we must also acknowledge that although we today adopt what we believe is a good tool for the police to succeed, it is not certain that we will get the desired result. But I have faith that the police are doing their best, and we politicians have to do our part in order for them to succeed as best they can. It must also be reflected in the budgets and in the increasing number of police officers and women. The change in structure gets a lot of attention — and there are important changes we are making there — but if we are to succeed in putting the police in a better position, the content we need to measure is: that more people get help when they need it, that fewer young people become criminals, that more things are solved, that terror is controlled, and that preparedness is strengthened. But as the Gjørv Commission pointed out, not everything can be adopted politically. We can facilitate a change in culture, but we cannot decide with a spherical point that culture, attitudes and leadership need to be improved – it needs to be done in an interaction. I am pleased that today we are adopting a police reform that has taken this criticism seriously, that we are adopting specific requirements for management competence at all levels of the police, that mentoring for police leaders is being introduced, that a Nordic degree of police leadership should be established – to mention something. These are important and good measures. If we are to build a good society, it is absolutely vital that we learn from our mistakes – that is also the case with the police. Therefore, it is essential that the evaluation in the course of its evaluation contributes to learning. The Christian People's Party has been given a clear mark on the reform through the negotiations. Among other things, I am glad that we have had a positive impact on the police present in all the municipalities of the country. All municipalities shall now have a police contact which shall be present one or more days a week. Local knowledge and humanities are important prerequisites for prevention, and the police must be present locally, and not centrally. At the same time, crime prevention must also be anchored at management level, and the Christian People's Party is pleased that there is now to be a professional or wise police officer in each police district dedicated to prevention. I would like to make it clear that if we are to ensure that prevention becomes a central element of police strategic thinking, this leader must have full-time work on prevention. Local anchoring in case of changes to the service structure is essential to ensure a good process. The municipalities concerned will make an active contribution to this work, and their voice will weigh heavily if an office is to be closed. Local participation is important when designing the police of the future – in this way we guarantee a police close to the people. Together, we have developed important criteria for the design of this structure. Not least, we should finally get a call for police response time. The police are to be present and operate the important prevention, but citizens must also be sure that the police are fast on the ground if anything happens. This will provide security. In order to ensure a safe and good society, it is not just the services that need to be disseminated. It is important that the specialised environments of competence are not just centralised around the police officer. We therefore agree that the professional communities should be spread in the police districts. It is important because it ensures quality throughout the country, helps to spread jobs and thus also ensures recruitment to the police from all over the country. The crime scene has changed in recent years. There is less crime, but crime has become more serious and organized. Then it is absolutely essential that the police be able to investigate and solve this type of crime. We need to ensure incentives for recruiting skilled police and others with acute skills that are needed to investigate and combat today's crime scene. It could be computer engineers, socionomists, economists, etc. It is very positive that we now have a national action plan in place to strengthen research. It is essential that the police are in the field and detect crime, but we also rely on the police to investigate and solve crime. Then it has to have a larger lift. The Christian People's Party is particularly pleased that there is a cross-political will to raise the competence and also the number of investigators working on violence in close relations, trafficking in human beings and sexual abuse. These are extremely serious issues, and it is crime that is extremely severe and therefore must be given priority. Unfortunately, we have more examples, even lately, of the fact that things in the police's work have gone wrong where things shouldn't have gone wrong. That's why I say again: we have to learn from the mistakes that are made – not to find scapegoats, but to make sure that it doesn't happen again. The police reform we are adopting today is based on the ten basic principles of the Police Committee. I am pleased that there is a consensus that the basic principles should stand firm, so that we get a police force to reflect the ideals of society, have a civil character, be a unitary police force, and that it should be decentralised. We thus ensure that the policeman should be a generalist and that the police should interact with the public and be integrated into the community. We ensure broad recruitment and that police focus their work on prevention. In particular, we ensure that the police are effectively controlled by society. It's crucial in a state of law. The police role committee pointed out that the basic principles contribute to ensuring small units, close cooperation with the public and integration into the community, and not least that prevention should be the primary objective of the police. With this foundation and the changes we are making today, we are securing a police force for the future.

Of course, it's July 22nd, when what wasn't supposed to happen happened, and we couldn't handle it in a way that we should, or that we could have wanted and expected. It's the Gjørv Commission that points to the weaknesses that were then, and then we have the police analysis, which gives us some recommendations on the way further in the work with the police. So this is no doubt that is a necessary reform to improve the police of the future. That's, of course, what the representative Ropstad was about, often the negative things we're hearing about, and often the negative things we're talking about. I also think it is important to stress that we have a very good police force in Norway today, and that there are many policemen out there doing a good job every day, which means a lot to the population, and that has a lot to say for the great trust the police have in the population. I can even tell sunshine stories from my meetings with different policemen. So I think it should also be said that when we are now discussing a reform with a serious backstop, there is a lot of good work going on out there. Preparedness is probably one of the most central issues in this reform, and it is precisely because of the back cover that has to do with July 22 in particular. This has been an important topic throughout the work. Because it's important that we improve. When we see how it failed on July 22nd, when we really should have set up as a society, there is a need to take some powerful action. But we can't just focus on the extreme events of July 22, we also have to focus on the preparedness to be serious, but despite all the more common events like that. natural disasters and other, more ordinary, things. For preparedness must be about a wide range of events, not just the most extreme cases. I believe that the reform, as it now stands, has a broader perspective than just the terrorist perspective, and I believe that it is a strength for the reform as it is now. Not least, I think it is good that we have focused on involving other resources as well, not least voluntary teams and organisations, which are a very important part of our preparedness work. Socket responsibility is an important part of the preparedness that we have along the coast. I am pleased that we are now stating that the base responsibility should continue to be divided into four districts and that today's location should be maintained. We already have good environments of competence, and I am pleased that, for example. Kristiansund will continue the important work in which they already have good competence. Prevention may be one of the most important things the police do. At the same time, there is some of the most difficult things to measure, and so it sometimes disappears in the debate. But I think we have raised this field up to where it belongs, with the focus that it should and must have. There are several ways out, but local knowledge and knowledge are essential for good prevention. Therefore, I am also very pleased that we point to the importance of local knowledge. The fact that, for example, All municipalities should have a regular police contact that follows them up especially, I think is a very good grip for prevention. Already today, there is a lot of good cooperation. SLT is an example of something that works very well and is based on very good local knowledge. Prevention is now being lifted even higher, and more emphasis will be placed on the work ahead. Although it is not the most important thing in the reform, it is often structure that ends up being what is discussed the most. I believe that the structure model that lay after the government and left agreed was a good model and a good starting point for the agreement that we have now concluded with the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party. There are some changes. Among other things, Finnmark is now assembled as one police district. Finnmark is in a very special situation because it is the police district that has the external border with Schengen, and that border issue is also important in the future. Finnmark also has a special situation with regard to the Sami problem, and there I think the police also have a task to do in the future. Among other things, the police do not have data tools that can handle Sami characters, such as: require them to enter notional names in the weapons register. I believe that when we get Finnmark together, we can also focus on that problem. That Asker and Bærum now have gone over to Oslo, is a solution that I perceive is wanted both in Oslo and in Asker and Bærum, so I think that's something everyone can live with. With regard to the Haugaland, which has perhaps been the most controversial and controversial locally, I think we have got a good solution, based on police counseling, and where preparedness is what is at the heart. Of course, when a billion-dollar infrastructure is built, it has to follow how we organize our society. So it should just be missing that there's going to be a change when Rogfast is in place. Then we have completely different communication options between Rogaland and Hordaland, not least for Haugalandand. Lensmen's offices are also some of the things that have been discussed a lot. For us, it has always been important that it should be a political responsibility at last to set up a Länsmannskontoret. But I think it's important that we give the police the opportunity to put down cannon offices, because one of the feedback we got on the previous police reform was that when we put down or merge districts, but get nothing done about the place structure, we prevent the police from making the changes that are needed to enable them to deliver the offer to the citizens that we expect them to deliver. Since we agreed with the Workers' Party and the Christian People's Party, we have been given a very thorough description of the process that will take place in advance of a merger through the closure of Länsmann's offices. I think that's useful for the police too, because it gives us clear expectations about how the municipalities and local communities should be involved in that process. It also helps to give legitimacy to the process and to the eventual closures that will and will have to come. In the end, I want to mention two things that aren't so often taken up in this context, because they don't appear to be very big when we discuss a whole reform. It's about smooth cells, and it's about 'psyche car'. I'm very happy that we're going to have a probationary system in at least one police district where overly drunk people aren't going to be put on smooth cell but transferred to health care. For me, it's about values. It's about how we treat people. If the Council of State is wondering if he's going to get some police districts to join such a trial scheme, then there's not something I'm wondering about, because I've already registered that the chief of police in today's Rogaland police district has been out in the media and said that this is something he wants to welcome, and at least Rogaland police district wants to be part of. I think that's very positive. ‘Psychiatric car’ is about the same thing – how we look at people who are mentally ill – it's about the health care system and not about the police.

I'm starting there, because the best counterworked crime is the one that doesn't happen. As many people know, there is an institution in society that has as slogans "We were there when it didn't happen" – it's not SV's new slogans, just to clarify it, it's its Natteramnana. She had a lot of positive intentions, but wasn't good enough. She didn't clearly define what the police were, what one should understand about it, and what one could expect in the future. She was in our view of centralizing and gave authority in important decisions to the office – away from democracy. She did not have sufficient resources to strengthen her research as a priority. And most importantly, she didn't have the total attention to culture and leadership that we expected would dominate. When something doesn't work in our administration, we may have added a habit of doing structural operations, at least it's often met that way. A change in limits, merges units, sets a new instruction, and adopts an action plan. It sounds good, it's easy to explain, and often it can be good. But the real changes happen in our heads and in our behavior. It is when something is in your head and hands – in the conversations every day, in the meeting that one has and in all the small and big choices one takes – that one really has got a change. That's what really is prioritizing something -- that one can call a culture. To give an example, issues of sexual assault, violence against children and violence in close relationships are a huge social problem that we have been able to deal with well enough so far. Historically, there have probably been large numbers of darkness, and we know that there are still a lot of things that are not reported, not cleared, and that one does not handle well enough. It is very good to have action plans against it; there are many necessary measures to put in place. But it's when there's a part of culture at all levels that it's important, that's when changes are really about to happen. On the basis of this, SV, together with others, called for, and subsequently initiated, discussions with the other party with a view to a broad consensus on changes in the proposed police reform. The discussions have led to several important changes: a clearer definition of local police, a number of important individual actions for culture and leadership, and a better appeal process for changes in service locations — to give some examples. However, the SV chose to withdraw during the final phase of these negotiations when it was clear that only adjustments to the proposed reform were made to the government. There were several important things we didn't get through for. Let me mention some of them. First of all, we proposed a big message or a more thorough work on culture and leadership that could go on over the years. As a government council, I have seen for myself how a staging message or another type of pervasive work can lead to an extensive process that gives a theme attention. This will be anchored in the layers below; they will do a big pre-work. The Department will launch a project, a work that would like to involve several departments. There will be listeners, recording rounds, learning from other countries and learning from the best examples. It will lead to a larger public debate, and it will eventually lead to a decision and a case in the Storting – which will also be anchored here. I have to say that I am still a little surprised that the government was not interested in meeting this. I think it's a requirement it should be possible to be a few of them. But it wasn't like that. The second thing I want to mention is that we still think it's the Department – that is, the Council of State, the Government – that's going to decide about service places. In principle, it is important in a democracy that it is the supreme political responsible who stands for decisions that are perceived as important. Then you will also take a broader view than just the ones you want to take. All decisions in important areas will be of concern, and the police are related to other matters. This is also the experience from other major government agencies, such as health and higher education, that in the end it becomes a political responsibility, so one might as well put the decision-making authorities there. We also thought that the final arrangement was too centralizing, there was too little willingness to make changes in the meeting with the Stortinget, for example. in the case of the number of police districts. Culture must not only be readable or expressed in the hierarchy structure – that the number of strips is the definition of leader, that the uniform defines the tasks, that the management or organisational chart is the only one that defines how matters should be resolved. Good management and a well-functioning structure are mostly about building up cooperation and actions that can also work when the external framework for tasks, content or management disappears. It's no longer like that, and we have a lot to learn from health care. Realising the cultural concept of ‘learning organisation’ is about looking for ‘what is in the walls’ and not so easy to discover for others. What has happened in the Hordaland police district is a serious alarm clock. Culture, with the background of this reform, can be moved from sitting in the walls to taking home the heads, hearts and hands of the many policemen who work every day to do an important job.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e1142	Police reform has had a long foreplay. This may reduce the risk of children and particularly vulnerable persons being subjected to further abuses or of the perpetrator being able to influence the victim in such a way that it does not dare to witness. In order to ensure cross-referencing for sighted persons, the suspect shall be given the opportunity to request additional questioning of the witness, where questions may be asked. The Government has proposed to introduce differentiated statutory deadlines for the conduct of hearings. In the most serious cases, the deadline shall be one week, with a deadline of two and three weeks for the remainder, taking into account the gravity of the case, against a deadline of two weeks for sexual assault today. Here the Workers' Party has a proposal for a equal deadline for everyone in a week, and I expect them here also to justify this for themselves. At the same time, I must stress that the introduction of statutory deadlines does not in itself ensure compliance with the deadline. The best interests of the child shall be the decisive importance of the assessment of the time for questioning and not the lack of resources. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e1620	I would like to thank the President-in-Office for putting us through this matter very well and for his commitment to such matters. The right is very pleased that the government has now delivered this important proposition on changing the criminal law. The proposal follows on from the report ‘Referral of particularly vulnerable persons in criminal matters’, in which the Code of Conduct on Judicial Hearing and Observation of Children and Psychiatric Depressed Persons was reviewed. This report was important to be implemented, precisely on the basis of experience gained after a few years of operation of children’s homes and now had the opportunity to gain operational experience and to propose improvements through such an evaluation. The Judicial Committee has also examined, through our period, proposals for representatives that have addressed parts of the content of this proposition, and the Right is very pleased with these changes that are now being promoted. The right is also pleased that, through the proposition, the government shows what we know, namely that they are concerned with ensuring our most vulnerable – those who are victims of abuse and violence – in the best possible way. Children’s homes were established in 2007, and we now have ten children’s homes around Norway to conduct and facilitate the interrogation and follow-up of children and adults with mental retardation or similar disability who have been subjected to violence and abuse or witnessed violence. When the Right-Fremskritspart government took over the government offices, the average waiting time for Questionnaires was 54 days. We all agree that there is too much time when the deadline is 14 days. This means that for many vulnerable people the waiting time was disproportionately long. Following pressure from the Minister of Justice, the police director has also provided a clear guidance signal that these matters should be prioritised, and in December 2014, the average waiting time was down to 32 days – still too long – and it is therefore important that this proposition be addressed now, in order to ensure changed procedures that can help reduce waiting time, so that we come within the new legal deadlines. The right is concerned with all the good measures that can help to help our most vulnerable. We are therefore very pleased with the proposal to remove the judge from the hearing and replace it with a lawyer. When the judge is out of the hearing, it will also be easier for the police to put up the work of questioning, and the right has a expectation that legal time limits for questioning can thus be more easily achieved. In particular, this change will also release judicial resources for other important tasks. The Court of Justice also supports the proposed amendment. The right is also pleased that the government clearly shows that they want to lower the threshold in order to report to the police and reduce the risk of child abuse and particularly vulnerable individuals. The proposal in the proposal opens up the opening of the first hearing, as a general rule, before the suspected intention or notification of the hearing. Due to the risk of evidence of abduction, and not least the possibility of an influence on the child, the government considers that it is unfortunate that the intention is made aware of the matter prior to the first questioning. The defendant and his rights are respected if it turns out that the police are proceeding with the matter. Finally, I would like to add a few words to the proposal to keep the age limit of 16, but extend it to 18 for those who are sexually assaulted by relatives. We have seen that there has been a huge increase in the number of reports of violence and abuse against children after the children's homes were established, and that is very good. At the same time, we see a challenge both in meeting the deadlines and in being able to provide the most vulnerable to follow-up and help they deserve and are entitled to. The right is therefore concerned that raising the age limit to 18 for all cases could be at the expense of those who need the most organised hearings. The right has a expectation that these proposed changes to the Criminal Procedures Act will contribute to reaching the new statutory deadlines, but of course supports the proposal here that, within two years – after assessing the impact of these changes – the government will review raising the age limit to 18 in all cases.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e1639	It is gratifying that the government, with good support, once again provides for the strengthening of victim care – now through a reform of children’s homes and interrogations. Firstly, it is now being ensured that the deadline for questioning the most vulnerable children is reduced to half of today’s requirements for two weeks. The most vulnerable children shall be given the right to questioning in the course of a week. This will be important in order to prevent further abuse of the emergency procedures. A differentiation of the deadlines of one to three weeks instead of a flat deadline of two weeks, which today, will ensure that the most serious issues could be prioritised more quickly than today. This will be particularly important, for example, in cases of sexual abuse in close relationships, where it is extremely important that the authorities can intervene at the earliest possible time. For children in vulnerable dependency situations, it is often essential that the suspected attacker is not informed that he or she is targeted, until the police have provided sufficient evidence to provide the necessary assistance to protect the child. The legislative proposal therefore contains an important reform, which ensures that targeted persons do not need to be informed until after the child has conducted interrogations and evidence is secured. This is a clear and important priority for children's rights in this type of case and a change The Progress Party has worked for too long. For the Advance Party, it has been important to protect the children from represals from an attacker and to ensure that evidence can be obtained through questioning without the intention being made aware of this immediately. He/she is given the opportunity to recommend a supplementary questioning of the child in retrospect so that legal certainty can be ensured. Interviews at children’s homes have often been delayed through formalities in the past. This is clearing up the government. Firstly, it will no longer be necessary to have a judge present in the hearings. The judge has himself stated that they are superfluous in the hearing situation, and has asked questions about their role in these situations. When such a formality, which is perceived as superfluous by the actors themselves, leads to a delay in the hearings, a reform is necessary, and I am pleased that it is in place. In addition, demands will now be made for all actors to appear at the time of the police’s decision, which will contribute to a further decline in treatment time. Guards will be set up similar to those we have in custody cases, which is surprising to me that the former government did not take the initiative itself. It is regrettable that unnecessary formalities and the low use of well-tested experience from other aspects of the legal system have not been taken into account in relation to the legal certainty of children. Fortunately, these problems are now being cleared up and legal certainty for children is being strengthened. I am pleased that the government also provides for a strengthening of the victims' rights in this case. I am pleased that a total accumulation is concerned about these matters and so clearly has spent time and focused on these matters, and I think we are many who will work hard in the future to strengthen this area further.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e1977	The starting point is, as many have already pointed out, that Norway was convicted in the European Court of Human Rights in the so-called Lindheim judgment, because the property protection of landowners under the European Convention on Human Rights was not adequately protected. We as legislator therefore have a responsibility to correct the bias of the law in order to ensure a better balance between landowners and parties. We were convicted because the fixing of a fast fixation fee for the extension of land fasteners resulted in a pre-allocation of parties. The proposal that is on the table today is going to correct this bias, and I must call it a demanding balance. The judgment of the EMD gives us that legislates a certain room of discretion. We need to find the right balance between the rights of landowners to property protection, while taking into account housing social considerations and the need for the predictability of parties. It is this balance Christian People's Party that we have found together with the Right, the Advance Party and the Left. I note that the central party is in favour of the government's original proposal, which ensures the landowner's greater return, while the Workers' Party has gone the other way and perhaps stretched the knit too far, so that Norway could again be convicted in the EMD. Today's case is both technical and complex, but at the end of the day this concerns the home of individuals and families. It is therefore absolutely central that the rules we adopt can now provide predictability for the individual and ensure a fair balance between the parties. The aim of the changes is precisely to prevent the break-up of the EMK, and we largely agree with the government's proposal except on two points. In order to ensure a good balance and, not least, predictability in regulating the fixation charge, it is central that the rate is set in the legislative text. It has been claimed by some that the rate should be set to 5 pst. This has shown both the EID and the hearing bodies with a financial background that landowners will generate a disproportionate return on the land value by extending the agreement. The government put forward a proposal for a rate of 2.5 pst, but this thought we could give the landowner too much of the change in value. In the Lindheim judgment, the party fee was less than 0.25 pst. of market value. In order to ensure a ‘fair balance’ between parties and landowners and because there is a lower risk to fasteners than other tenure conditions, on which the rapporteur was involved, we have, together with the Right, the Advance Party and the Left, believed that a more correct rate would be 2 pst. The working party on its side proposes a rate of 1.25 pst. I think it's a little daring, all the time it could lead to our being re-judged in the EMD. This rate has not been carefully investigated by the Department of the Law Department of the Ministry of Justice, but the Ministry has provided legal assistance on the design of a legislative proposal. The cooperation lots have also agreed to introduce a safety valve that allows both anchorages and landowners to re-regulate the fastening charge once at least 30 years have elapsed from the previous regulation. The background for this is to prevent a future imbalance in the contractual relationship. The working party has proposed to introduce a degressive roof for large plots. It is a proposal we have a great sense of. At the same time, as both Frölich and Leirstein have been involved, this has already been observed in case law, and the example used by the Workers' Party in the setting is not very precise. When setting a new fastening charge for large plots, it is not possible for one to multiply only the ‘roof’ by the number of decks the plot is. An assessment of each land must be carried out. Firstly, a deduction shall be made from the value added by the attachment to the plot and one shall deduct from the ‘inhabitable’ denarius. In a judgment, the Supreme Court has laid down principles for the assessment of the roughness value. Thus, there is a practice of degressive roofing as early as today’s practice. With the thorough opinions of the majority in this Chamber, it will also help and will therefore not require any legislative changes. Several have also been subject to the agricultural derogation. I agree that this matter is about correcting what we have been convicted of in the EMD, but I also want to express my dissatisfaction with the agricultural exception where some parties who feel locked up cannot redeem the land. That's why I want to inform you that there's something we'll be looking at, perhaps also incentivised to allow more people to redeem the plots and possibly look at the abolition of the entire scheme, but there's a debate that we have to come back to later. Now I am at least happy that we are making changes that will ensure that we correct the bias that we have, after all, been condemned for.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e1996	It is a very good, comprehensive and thorough work done in the Santa Claus Committee, which gave its position in 2013 on parties and international law. They have worked on solutions related to the fact that Norway was convicted in the Human Rights Court, and that was because the Santa Claus Act § 33 was designed so that it was not consistent with property protection. In this context, there is reason to recall that property rights are protected under Article 17 of the Convention on Human Rights and that was the starting point for this case. For the centre party, private ownership is a fundamental value, and we have for many years pointed to the imbalance in land-fixing legislation, where it can be simplified to say that, as the rules have been designed, land-holdings have received the largest proportion of the capital increase on a land that they do not own, while the owner of the land has not received a reasonable share of the capital increase on the land. It was this imbalance that the Human Rights Court also pointed out in 2012. Santa party is a special kind of rental: you own the house that is set on the plot, but you don't actually own the plot itself. Some people have thought that the ownership of the land has changed over time to parties. That's not the case. Santa Claus is actually and legally a tenancy relationship, and so it has also been dealt with in the Human Rights Court. The government's proposal aims to balance the relationship between landowners and landowners, so that our national rules are compatible with the views of the Human Rights Court. The lot agrees that changes in the fastening charge must be made to ensure that land appreciation falls to both parties. It seems that the government believes that a rate of 5 pst. of the crude turbidity value is too high, while 2,5 pst. is taken as a reasonable balance between the parties. Sense if someone thinks that a maximum rate of NOK 11 378 might seem high, for example. for a plot of 4 dimensions, it should be borne in mind that most fixators will have the right to a redemption of the plot at 25 times the fixation fee, or 40 pst. of the crude turbidity value. In theory, this means that you could have redeemed the land, split the land and then sold with quite a lot of profit in areas of the country where the housing market is under pressure. Many of us would also have had to pay far more for the purchase of a common land than a party would have to pay to obtain a land based on today's redemption rules. The centre party is part of the broad majority who wish to re-regulate after 30 years. We're looking at it as a safety valve, and we're also happy that the so-called agricultural exemption will pass. With this I take up the proposal of the Centre Party.

The cases of the Storting today shall be settled, the so-called first control station, do not involve major changes to the electrical certification system, and this is wise. Stability and predictability are important for those who are to invest under the scheme. The Government’s proposal extends the time limit for new installations to be in service in order to be entitled to an electricity certificate until 31 December 2021. In this way, good projects planned to start in 2020 could be realised without uncertainty in order to comply with the deadline. The whole of the Committee has given its assent to this amendment. The Committee also agrees that the next inspection post should be accelerated to 2017 instead of 2019, as it should have been under the original agreement with Sweden. In this way, any legislative changes could enter into force on 1 January 2018 and become relevant to the achievement of the target within the deadline of 2021. I am pleased that the transitional electricity certificate scheme will also include works built in 2004 or later. The Labour Party, SV and the Environmental Party The Greens do not want to give these works the right to an electricity certificate, and I expect that they will justify their views in subsequent speeches during the debate. But the majority see it in such a way that it is about fair treatment, and it is about small-scale power stations that are currently struggling, but which have the potential to ensure just increased renewable energy production. The centre party is also in favour of a further extension of the transitional regime to a few works, which, following the amendment adopted by the Stortinget today, will remain out of the scheme. In the case of works above 10 MW built before 7 September 2009 as well as installations with increased generation as a result of upgrading, which should also be included in the transitional electricity certificate scheme. Norway and Sweden have conducted negotiations on changes to the single market agreement for an electricity certificate, and the result is that the goal of new electricity production is now 28.4 TWh. However, the agreement depends on the Swedish government meeting the condition to reduce the tax benefits for Swedish wind power by removing the exemption from wind power that is not supplied professionally. There is some uncertainty about what will be done on the Swedish side in this way, and I stress that the Riksdag must take such a decision, if not the whole agreement could fall away, which the Council of State has also confirmed to the Stortinget. The last week has been dramatic in an energy policy perspective. State power has been informed that they are withdrawing from wind power projects in the Nordic region, and the consequence is that Norway’s remote largest renewable effort is at risk of not being realised. Wind power development at Fosen is one of the largest in the world, with total investment of between 15 and 20 billion. kr. We in the Center Party ask ourselves what the consequence is for the national renewables effort and the target of 67.5 pst. renewable share in 2020 if this project is not realised. There is also reason to ask what happens to the objective of becoming a leader in environmental technologies and less dependent on oil. I also record that Statnett is now creating uncertainty about how to build a high-voltage line from Namsos to Surnadal, and whether it will be realised at all. It will be serious both in relation to the renewable target and because the Norwegian society is experiencing falling profitability in the oil industry and increasing unemployment. We need a green transition for many reasons, and there must be a willingness to bet and risk making Norway a major renewable producer, even outside hydropower. This is going to be an important aspect of today's debate. There are many questions that need to be answered. That is why the debate has also expanded, so that aspects of what has happened can be highlighted through the debate.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e2455	We're dealing with one thing with a long history today. Green certificates, or electricity certificates, were intended as a market-based support system to accelerate the deployment of renewable energy sources. Renewable energy producers would receive revenues from the sale of a certificate to consumers in addition to the sale of the electricity. An important additional argument, seen in the light of the 2003 power crisis, was to ensure that increased hydropower deployment would contribute to increased security of electricity supply through the winter months. The scheme was adopted by the Government Parties of the Bondevik II Government, together with SV, with a government setting up such a scheme with a single Norwegian-Swedish market for green certificates by the end of 2005. The current government had the aim of getting the scheme up and running, but this was only postponed because one in Sweden wanted to postpone the start-up. The Swedish already had such a market to be adapted to a common Norwegian-Swedish market. A one-sided Norwegian market would be too small to be an effective tool for increasing renewable energy production. It is quite right – perhaps no one has said it, but I have sometimes said it – that we, the Norwegian Government, were not very concerned about increasing the ambitions of the common Norwegian-Swedish certification system from 26.4 TWh to 28.4 TWh, but based on the fact that over the years we have had a long and trusting and good cooperation with our Swedish colleagues, regardless of what government we have had in Norway – and in Sweden, for that matter – we tried to be present with the new Swedish government. They could have implemented this goal on their own and created exactly the same market challenge without including it in the agreement, so we used the opportunity to negotiate with the Swedes to remove the electricity tax exemption that has previously been applicable to wind power, heavy wind power, in Sweden. We were very concerned about creating more competitive conditions between Norwegian and Swedish large wind power projects. The proposal of the Swedish Government to the Riksdagen went much further than what we have asked for, and I understand that this is what has been the debate in the Swedish word change and among Swedish parliamentarians, not what we have asked for, namely that the electricity tax exemption too much wind power should be removed. So I have noted that the Swedish government has now chosen to withdraw the first proposition and has said that they will return to Riksdag in two different propositions, raising the level of the electricity certification scheme in one case and abolishing the electricity tax exemption in another case. What many, including the case rapporteur, have been pointing out, is that of course it is a prerequisite for the whole agreement that all parts are followed up by both countries. Then it was referred to the energy policy of Tord Lien. Now, Tord Lien doesn't have a private energy policy, even though a lot could suggest this in place. Tord Lien is trying as hard as he can to control the government's energy policy. But that's the way the certification system is set up by the red-green ones, along with Sweden. I am pleased that the red-green parties are essentially ending up with the changes we are discussing today. So we're removing the uncertainty moment for a plant that might have been designed to be completed in October 2020, but will be completed in March 2021. With the regime that existed before we took over, a plant that was completed in March 2021 would not have a single ear in green certificate receipts. The second is that we have helped the Swedish authorities to remove the exemption from the electricity charge for large wind turbines. We have strengthened access for these future producers to the European market and thus strengthened profitability. We have said that we are going to introduce a depreciation system that will result in a five-year depreciation period also on Norwegian wind power projects, which have been carried out in Sweden in recent years, and which have contributed greatly to the fact that a good part of the plants have ended up in Sweden. On a number of points, we have strengthened the situation for the development of renewable energy projects, and therefore there are two things I am pleased about. One is that there is still one in four actors on the Fosen who have said that this project is dead, that they do not want to go further with the project. Therefore, I do not think that the representatives of Storting should declare this project buried. In fact, there are still some forces that work to realize this big project. That it's gotten harder because Statkraft has gone out of it, I don't think anyone should hide on it, but it's still working on it, and it can still be realized. It is claimed that we are not working to replace fossil energy with renewable energy. That's just not right. On the contrary, we have significantly strengthened the instrument that is primarily responsible for facilitating increased use of force in Norwegian society, for example. Enova, who has also taken over all responsibility for electrifying and reducing CO2 emissions from transport. We have worked to replace fossil heat with renewable energy sources, we have strengthened strong efforts to reduce CO2 emissions and improve energy efficiency in industry, which strengthens the competitiveness of industry, and Johan Sverdrup will be supplied with power from land, the largest electrification in Norwegian history – all of this under the leadership of this government.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e2784	This case concerns the submission by the Ombudsman’s defence department of the activities from 1 January to 31 December 2014. The Ombudsman’s defence title shall, following the instructions adopted by the Grand Duchy of Great Britain, contribute to ensuring the general human rights of the defence personnel. The activities of the Ombudsman shall also contribute to the effectiveness of defence. As part of the function of the Ombudsman, five inspections were carried out by the Ombudsman in 2014 and the following were carried out: War School of Army at Linderud in Oslo Air Force School Centre at Kjevik by Kristiansand 139 Luftving / Bardufoss Air Station in Målselv 131 Luftving / Air Force Station in Sørreisa Hans Majestet Kongens Garde at Huseby in Oslo The Committee has noted that the Ombudsman’s views have been very positive. They have experienced good managers with clear attitudes, a strong willingness to work and a good working environment. This was particularly demonstrated by the trustees of the departments, both employees and guardians. It is very gratifying to hear about both good management and good work environment, as these are the main prerequisites for producing good services. For 2014, the Ombudsman has identified five areas which will give particular attention to: One is property, construction and construction, or EBA, which is often referred to as a collective term. In some central installations, the building mass continues to be experienced as worn-out and inappropriate, which, in turn, is a heavy burden for the personnel using the buildings in question. However, a number of required modernisations have been made in the case of cablings, fairs, etc. On the part of the Committee, it is assumed that the defence will remain under pressure in this area. Another theme is the introduction of a general protection obligation from 2015. In order to succeed, there is a need for additional efforts, as in several establishments there are congestion and also significant gaps. There are several closet rooms, wardrobes and sanitary rooms that have to be arranged so that both sexes have satisfactory living conditions. Some temporary adjustments have been made, but it goes without saying that this is not a sustainable situation in the long run. Another aspect is the provision of equipment adapted to women, both for personal clothing and equipment. The Committee expects the defence to follow up on this. Still changes in the defence complicate recruitment, especially by skills-requiring personnel. The Committee is concerned about this, especially because the defence uses advanced weapon systems for which it takes a long time to train and upgrade personnel. The Committee therefore assumes that measures are taken to maintain critically important personnel specifically trained to ensure the defence weapon systems. The Committee has noted that the Ministry of Defence has decided to launch the clean-up service on a competitive basis. The Committee assumes that the rights of its servants are properly safeguarded by the defence when this task is transferred to others. Like the Ombudsman, the Committee would point out that the competitive outsourcing of this service can address some security challenges, particularly in the case of vulnerable sites. It is therefore assumed that this will be given special attention in the planning of the competitive environment. In the past, the Ombudsman has pointed to various weaknesses in the treatment and support of our veterans. In the 2014 reporting, this theme is highlighted, and I quote: ‘The Ombudsman’s subject is pleased with the considerable work being constantly strengthened, and that as a nation, we are increasingly recognising the important work done and done by Norwegian personnel. The last action plan shows a number of significant points and mechanisms that contribute to increasing the attention and awareness of veterans and their environment.’ I find it appropriate to give honor to the Defense for having taken hold of this area, so that we now see an improvement in the work of caring for our veterans. As a final point, I would like to dwell on the work on security clearance of defence personnel. In addition to the Ombudsman’s mention of this as one of his focus themes in his annual report, the Ombudsman’s task was specifically attributed to the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee in his own letter. Between 8000 and 9000 women and men serve the first service of defence. Of the tasks to be carried out, approx. 90 pst. of such a nature that the personnel required to carry them out must be cleared for safety. In the case of soldiers with dual citizenship and soldiers with parents of foreign origin, it has long been a problem to clear them up. Collecting data from abroad is a problem, and especially from nations we do not cooperate with. But even for ethnic Norwegians, there are today problems related to the time it takes to obtain security clearance. For orders that have lost the security clearance, the problem is even greater if possible. In the meantime, they are not allowed to carry out the tasks they are employed to do. Any person may imagine the load on the personnel and a slow safety clearance may have a negative impact on the general breakdown of the protection obligation. This in itself is serious. Once the Ombudsman’s defence subject has received a complaint from several officers who lost their clearance in 2013 and who have still not received a final decision, there is something completely wrong with this service. The Committee refers to the fact that the defence Ombudsman has raised this matter on several occasions with the National Safety Authority and the Defence Security Department, without having resulted in a significant improvement in the duration of proceedings. As this has been a recurring issue, last year the Committee asked the Ombudsman to report on developments in the security clearance of personnel, and it is discouraging that this year, too, the Committee has to report that there has been no significant improvement in this situation. It is now the Information and Security Committee, EOS, which is dealing with complaints concerning security clearance, that is why I also refer to the next case with similar comments on security clearance, in the setting of Document 7:1, which concerns the Annual Report of the EOS Committee for 2014 and which addresses the same issue of security clearance as in this case. The Committee now expects the issue of security clearance to be addressed seriously and the provision of sufficient capacity for NSM and the FSA, possibly providing other improvement measures to ensure that the work on security clearance of soldiers and defence personnel is done in a good and rational manner. A review of how other safety authorities perform this type of service should also be included. Today's service, it can't go on. I refer you to the Committee's comments and attitude, and I recommend this committee's unanimous vote.
