ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e3019	This article will fall into three parts – first a comment on the Committee setting, which, despite a unanimous opening around the comments, also deserves some deepening. I can agree with the supplementary statement that our permanent case rapporteur for the EOS Committee had, and would just say that we agree from the Right that a very central point to build trust is that what is going on in the monitoring process is grounded, and that you stay within the limits set by this hall through the law. Then I will also join in what was said about the processing time in classification matters, and that otherwise the annual report has pointed to the need for changes in the control law and the control instructions. Then I can also inform you that the Right will be in a positive position to respond to the proposals made by the EOS Committee. We regard them as factually justified and primarily of a technical nature. As for international developments, at the end of May I had the opportunity to attend an interparliamentary conference in Brussels. Normally it's the EEA that brings us there, but this time it was EOS. There was an exchange of experience between European parliaments on how to ensure full democratic control with something by definition hidden. We recognized each other's working conditions and efforts to bring services under democratic control, but very different organisational forms were chosen. We are also told that Norway was among the few who had chosen this scheme, with a sample reporting directly to the national Parliament. There were some who probably found it safest that such control authorities report best to governments, who are better off keeping secrets than national parliaments, but then, of course, is a very obvious counter-argument that the government is also very closely interwoven with the executive. So there was a number of interest in the Norwegian model in this context. And of course, there were also objections that maybe you weren't so sincere to a national parliament that there were very general reports that couldn't really provide full information. But that's where we can answer that the EOS Committee, which is made up of people with a social background, insight and not least integrity, who, on behalf of the usual and official control mechanisms we know, such as transparency, searchability and a wake-up press, in these questions are given our confidence to peer these services into the cards and report back in a way that does not compromise the services, their methods or employees. You can also say, after many years of business with this model, that the Storting has never had reason to express disappointment at the way the EOS Committee has worked. So it is a compliment to the people who have been sitting up over the years in these very important positions and have done an important job for Parliament – in our country, the Stortinget – and for democracy. So all the legal states are in the same dilemma: how are we to combat hidden threats to the state using instruments that cannot be reported continuously in real time – as it is now called – without undermining the purpose of the work? The third thing I would like to mention in this little post is what the control function of the EOS committee should be. They shall fulfil three very important functions: they shall address possible excesses. Secondly, they shall ensure that all the secret services have a safe threshold to pass in order to initiate intervention against individuals in Norway. The third is to ensure that there is a proportionality in the use of instruments, i.e.: that consideration is also given to the backstopping of hazards, risks and harmfulness to the interventions that may potentially need to be addressed by individuals, and that alternative ways that may be less intrusive, perhaps more demanding, are also considered. It's also in a proportionality assessment. These are difficult questions to weigh, and therefore it is also quite essential that we have made it clear that the EOS Committee is not alone. In addition to the controls carried out by the EOS Committee – which, of course, must be based on reports of what has happened – we also have a very good judicial system, which we can probably discuss about getting even better. However, the advantage of the judicial system is that it is well-tested, that it is used to handling also secret information, and that it can be linked at any time by the procedure — by having mechanisms when assessing the measures to be taken, and it may be to check whether you have the right legal basis along the way. And of course, the court can also finally do some of what the EOS committee does, that's a back-up. That's why, on this occasion too, since two of the speakers were referring to a suggestion about PST – and you can of course refer to PST in an EOS debate, it's okay – that means that what we're going to be careful about is now having too strong opinions about what PST has had for reasons or not reasons to go into the matter that's been mentioned in the media. It can't be mature to do it now; on the contrary, it can be quite premature. And that's because I've also seen the newspapers that a representative would like to have the country's Minister of Justice – under parliamentary information responsibility to the Stortinget – explain what the reason for a special seizure might be. This is not the way a parliament can operate, but that is why we have the EOS Committee. They read newspapers, and they have said in several reports that they keep themselves informed about the media image. They are not more buried in public documents than they get the whole picture and on behalf of the public, on behalf of the press, do the job. So I want to strongly warn you that we as single representatives are going in and now do the job that we have chosen the EOS Committee to do. It's not because we have to discharge ourselves from responsibility, but we're lawmakers, lawmakers, and we can't operate alternative, parallel legal application. I think we agree on that with more thought.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e3050	We see a changed security policy situation. At a time of unrest and uncertainty, it is essential that we have functioning EOS services to ensure our national security. To meet the challenges we face, it is important that the EOS service has the necessary tools and space for action. Similarly, it is important that we have democratic control over our secret services. The EOS Committee shall carry out an independent review and verification to ensure that the service complies with the established legislation. I am therefore pleased that the sample in the Annual Report has described that the EOS service has consistently demonstrated an understanding of the control of the sample in 2014. I agree with the sample that the control helps to ensure the legal certainty of individuals and to create confidence that the service operates within its legal framework. In 2011, 2012 and 2013, the Committee and the Committee criticised the lengthy process of clearance in the defence sector. The committee has requested the Stortinget to deal with this matter since the trial period is so long that it leads to a disproportionate intervention in the lives of individuals. The Committee has asked the Ministry of Defence to take immediate action, and I can assure you that action has been taken from autumn 2014 until now, and we will continue to do so. I share the deep concern of the committee and committee and take it very seriously. With the President's permission, I will now, as I announced in the previous case, spend some time saying something about status and action. First, a little bit about architecture. The FSA is responsible for the security clearance of the Armed Forces personnel. The national safety authority, NSM, is the complaint authority of the clearing authorities. At the same time, they have national responsibility for clearing at the highest clearance level, the Cosmic Top Secret – CTS. The Ministry of Defence is the complaint authority in the CTS cases and the clearing authority for foreign nationals in the sector. There have been long-term challenges for several years. One of the reasons is that matters are far more complex than before. For example, both a globalised labour market and immigration contribute to the links of many with other countries. Of course, the nature of such an association must be assessed on a case-by-case basis. It's time-consuming. It is also time-consuming to obtain personal data from cooperating countries, which I had so much to say in previous articles. The volume of cases is also a challenge. For example, the number of Cosmic Top Secret cases increased from 1 000 applications to 1 750 applications between 2012 and 2013. I have therefore started a review of the background to this increase. It may be that clearance is requested at a higher level than is really needed. Additional security clearances may also be required. The Defence Security Department deals with between 18 000 and 20,000 cases a year. It is obvious that the effective handling of so many cases depends on a good procedural tool. We therefore had great expectations for the introduction of the new full-electronic process tool Mimir. Unfortunately, one of the reasons for the disproportionately long procedure period in 2014 is the very introduction of Mimir. When Mimir was put into service late summer 2014, it quickly became clear that the system had major defects and deficiencies. There was a sharp increase in the number of cases pending in the clearance authorities in a short time. So the effort to repair Mimir started quickly. The result is that Mimir today works at an acceptable level. Compared to the situation facing the clearing house last autumn, this is a positive development which will help to reduce the length of proceedings. At the same time, the lack of human resources contributed to the long process. I have therefore given leadership both to the Defence and to the National Safety Authority to give priority to the work on safety clearances, in order to reduce the length of the procedure. It has been done both through PET, i.e. clarifications, changes and additions to the implementation letters, and through the etat management dialog. To date, the Defence Security Department has been strengthened by three new annual works this year and a further five will be provided in a short time. This means that the Defence Security Department will have increased the number of case handlers from 17 to 25 in the current year. In addition, two years' work has been assigned to the Defence Personnel and Security Centre in order to ensure the role of the requesting authority, thereby ensuring a better flow of cases between the Centre and the Defence Security Department. For information, the Defence Security Department reduced the residue list from the highest of 7,420 cases in February this year to 3,660 cases today. It's a positive development. Since autumn 2014, the national safety authority has allocated resources to the clearing authority to reduce the residues. In addition, they are strengthened by four new job homes. That means they're now dealing with things faster than new things come in. Overall, NSM’s procedural capacity has been strengthened from 8 to 13 case handlers. The national safety authority has already been able to reduce the process duration of the CTS cases. At the last reporting in May, it was 102 days and the objective is a maximum of 90 days. Both the Defence Security Department and the National Security Authority have now been provided with resources to significantly strengthen their procedural capacity. I expect both FSA and NSM to reduce the procedure during 2015, but will therefore follow these developments very closely. Both the Defence and the NSM state that they will provide more case handlers if necessary. I have also made clear that they must request additional resources if they see that the need is there. They have reported that it is not yet. The autumn of 2014 changed the defence procedures for calling and distributing to the first time service, as I mentioned in the previous case. This was a practice I stopped right away from the Ministry of Defence because it is not in line with the legislation, namely that individual treatment should be ensured. One of the conclusions of the working group that was subsequently decommissioned by the head of defence was that the clearing process should start earlier, so as to clarify the clearing issue before the first duty. In the autumn of 2015, a pilot project will be launched at the session centre in Kjevik, where this scheme will be tested. This means that for all – regardless of connection – allocated to the initial service, the clearing process should start immediately. Of those who received this letter from the Defense in the autumn of 2014, who – as I mentioned – have now received a new letter, and who wanted to join in, no one today has been cleared up for security. It's quite natural, because there's only a few of them that have so far completed the part of the session process that comes ahead of the clearing process. At the same time, the defence needs to clear up the security of personnel, including those entering the initial service. There will still be some who do not receive safety clearance, but it will then be based on an individual assessment, and that is normal practice. In this context, it is important to point out that the EOS Committee is reviewing the issues in which safety clearance is not provided. Three times a year, the Committee inspects the Defence Security Department. The committee has not commented on this kind of issue in the 2014 Annual Report. The Committee has pointed out in the Annual Report that some types of clearing cases are treated differently by different clearing authorities. This is unfortunate. On 20 May this year I sent a proposal for amendments to the Safety Act to be consulted. In the draft consultation, there are, among other things: proposed to significantly reduce the number of clearing authorities. It is likely to contribute to a greater level of non-discrimination and increase the quality of clearing decisions. It will also simplify the control and oversight role of the EOS Committee and provide clear economies of scale. In the Annual Communication, the sample pointed out the need for an external evaluation of the implementation of security calls. I have also noticed that the Committee feels that there is a need for such an external evaluation and that it is important that this work be prioritised. Security calls are based on a variant of the conversation technique called KREATIV, developed by the Norwegian police. CREATIV is based on the PEACE model. The methodology of the PEACE model is widely recognised and used, among others. by the police, the Foreign Affairs Directorate and the Resumption Commission. The EOS Committee and the National Safety Authority are still in dialogue on the need for an external evaluation. I am concerned that the NSM priority is to continue this dialogue with the sample. I have noticed that the sample in the Annual Report comments that the quality of the implementation of the security calls varies across the different clearing authorities. There could always be an improvement in the performance of some calls. It is therefore important that we reduce the number of clearance authorities, so that we get fewer and stronger professional authorities with increased specialisation and competence. The EOS Committee has noted in the Annual Report that they need access to Mimir. The National Safety Authority is now working to facilitate the establishment of a dedicated room for the EOS Committee at the NSM premises. There are resources allocated to it, and the work is in progress. When the EOS Committee has described that the EOS services are continuously facilitating the monitoring of the sample, I will specifically refer to the extensive work carried out in the E-service with the conversion of the entire archive system. This has enabled the sample to carry out searches as requested by the sample and in a way that is more consistent with the understanding of independent control. This contributes in the way that we rely on trust. I am also pleased that the sample specifies the positive in the Annual Report. In the 2013 Annual Communication, the sample asked questions about the basis for the processing of sensitive personal data by third parties related to source use. The question arose in connection with the sample survey of Norwegian sources in 2013, the so-called trade archive case. The Ministry has made an in-depth assessment of the legal basis, which the sample indicates has been taken for information purposes. The selection also specifies that they will continue to control the service in this area. This is an important part of the control to be carried out by the sample. I am pleased that the sample also specifies in this context that the control work is simpler as a result of the organisation of the E-service. In the annual report, the sample shows that they are expecting the Storting to address the issue of the transparency of the sample in the particular sensitive information of the E-service. Assessment of the current mechanism is part of the mandate of the evaluation committee. The Ministry of Defence has assisted the evaluation committee with information they have requested, among others. with regard to the need to maintain the system, as laid down by the Stortinget in 1999, which was confirmed in 2009 and which has been maintained by all Ministers of Defence since its inception by Jørgen Kosmo. Although the E-service has been loyal to current legal understanding and the decision of the Stortinge, it is challenging that the current scheme be constantly challenged. I look forward to the Storting's clarification in this matter. In their annual report, the EOS Committee describes one complaint case where they have found reasons to criticise the E-service. I would first like to make it clear that I am very serious that the sample is considering criticising the service for relations related to the information collection of Norwegian citizens located in Norway. I am aware that the E-service has also taken this matter as seriously and has taken immediate action to prevent similar situations from occurring. However, it is important for me to point out that no information has been collected on Norwegian citizens contrary to the legislation. I would also like to be clear that the case has its background in an error in the internal information flow in the service, so it is not a deliberate breach of the laws and regulations to which the service is subject. What happened was that information from PST that the person had returned to Norway had by mistake not been captured in the E-service. I will follow this up with the E service and ensure that the necessary actions are in place to ensure that internal processes and procedures for information management are good enough. Finally, I have noticed that the EOS Committee has described that in 2014 they have devoted considerable resources to organising the evaluation committee. It will continue in 2015. It is important for the democratic control and legitimacy of the secret services that, in line with other developments, the activities and framework conditions of the EOS Committee are also taken into account.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e3125	The Civil Ombudsman is an important institution in Norwegian society. He shall ensure that the administration takes care of its individual interests in a complex administration, in a complex society. Through the Civilo Ombudsman, the individual makes it possible for an administrative decision to be examined by a neutral and independent body. The Civil Ombudsman also makes an important effort to monitor and control the respect and security of human rights. It is therefore an important part of Storting's work to deal with the annual report of the Civilo Ombudsman. I would like to draw up a few points from the 2014 Annual Report, which are also mentioned in the Committee's comments. Nav is a theme that is left in the annual news from the Civilo Ombudsman. In 2014, the Civilombodsman criticised the administration in three cases of legal security relating to parts of Nav’s legislation, which includes activising measures and performances to get the most involved in the work. The cases came in the light of changes in the promises that followed in the wake of the Nav reform. It is worth noting that the Civilombodsman points out the importance of the department conducting thorough analysis of the consequences for the legal certainty of this type of bill, and that the analyses are presented to the Stortinget. There is also something the Stortinget should take with him in his further work. Another theme that came up again in the annual report is sanitary conditions in prison. In the light of the debate we have recently had on the capacity of criminal services, an own message here in the Stortinget, and the maintenance needs of Norwegian prisons, there is a criticism that authorities should follow up. In addition, it is worth noting that for many years, the Civilobodsman has been indicting violations of the main rule of transfer from police detention to prison within two days. Oversight in police arrest is a problem that needs to be taken seriously. Over the last few years, there has been an increase in the number of overdoses in the Oslo police district, while at the same time the coverage of criminal services is very high and the settlement queues are increasing. Long sitting time in police detention has been the subject of criticism from the Council of Europe's torture prevention committee, and it is a theme that we are forced to take more seriously in the Norwegian community. The International Police Office at Trandum is also a theme mentioned in the Annual Report. It is positive that the material conditions at the boarding school have been better, but there is cause for concern when the Civilo Ombudsman felt that control and security had been over-implemented in comparison with the concerns of those who internalized their private lives in some areas. In the Civil Ombudsman’s statements, it is made, among other things: highlighted: ‘Full body vision with regard to the abdomen is a very invasive measure.’ In-house routine body searches were often and unexpectedly at risk, but there appeared to be no concrete assessments of the need for individual body searches. It follows from the case-law of the European Court of Human Rights that it may constitute an infringement of Article 3 of the European Convention on Human Rights. We expect the government to follow up on those concerns. A last theme I want to mention, which has also been left in the annual reports, and which is a theme this time as well, is the freedom of speech to add. Freedom of expression is a fundamental human right, and rights in Norway are protected by the Constitution. Initially, the same freedom of expression has been added to the freedom of expression as others, including at work, but it may be added that it may be subject to some legitimate limitations linked to the obligation to participate and the obligation of non-statutory loyalty. The Civil Ombudsman emphasises that such an obligation to act loyally against the enterprise in which one is assigned does not mean that the employer can regulate or sanction statements that add on his own behalf on political matters, and points in the Annual Report to a specific case from 2014, where this theme was addressed. It is, I would say, essentially to ensure the addition of its freedom of expression, and it is a theme that has been at the forefront of several things in recent years. It is crucial for an informed public debate. Additions may have specific competence, insight and experience in a relevant field and may also contribute to the detection of critical issues. In the light of the Civilombodsman’s concerns in the previous annual report, this is a field that should be covered by special attention in the future. With these comments, I conclude my work, as a case spokesman, with the annual report and reiterate that it is an important work, and that it is important that the Stortinge is well informed about it.

Earlier this week, the Stortinget has hopefully helped to resolve some of this situation by providing 242 new prison sites, hopefully from 1 September onwards and correctly in the Netherlands, but this will still help to increase the possibility of faster transfer to ordinary custody zones and thus also help to reduce congestion, as well as to address some of the other challenges in the area of criminal care. But this is something we have a lot of focus on. If you look at it historically, in 2009 there were 3,539 cases of overdraft, according to figures from the Police Directorate. In 2013, there were 4,250 cases of the same thing. So there's been an increasing trend except for a slight decrease in 2011. In 2014, the figure was 3,440, according to the figures we received from the Police Directorate. There are large numbers, and it is necessary to have the necessary political pressure on this, but it is also necessary to ensure that the agencies are able to solve this better in the future. Then I will also mention the issues related to Trandum, where there is a balance between security issues and privacy. There's, as I'm told, the PU is doing a review to make sure that one is within the limits that one should be. It is of course necessary, as the chairman of the committee also pointed out, that we should not go beyond the limits of human rights here.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-10.ana.ud68e3211	The Civil Ombudsman’s preventive unit against torture and inhumane treatment in the event of a loss of freedom was created in spring 2014 and a central purpose of the creation of the unit is to fulfil the obligations assumed by Norway as a result of the accession to the Additional Protocol to the Torture Convention, often abbreviated to the OPCAT, following the original English-language title. The pre-convention unit has the right to visit all places where people are, or may be, outside its freedom. The aim of the visit is to prevent torture and other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or punishment. Visits may be alerted or unannounced. The committee has noticed that the device has used the phase from creation to get started. Many of the work done in 2014 has focused on planning, the development of working methods and procedures, and the creation of documents such as visit manuals and priority criteria for where to investigate. In addition, there have been more concrete visits and dialogue and contacts with a number of actors – from public authorities, educational institutions, trade unions and academia to civil society. A total of five visits were carried out by the unit during 2014. Of them, two were in prison and three were in police custody. The visited institutions have received a report with recommendations after the visit, and we have argued that these recommendations will be followed up. We expect that the prevention unit will carry out clearly more visits in 2015 than in 2014, as the first year more or less without exception took place for start-up and launch. The Committee has noted that the prevention unit notes that high levels of coating and building and manning challenges lead to increased isolation and tightening when it is related to joint detention. A joint committee is concerned about that. We are also concerned that the prevention unit has the impression that female intervention is getting a little-satisfying condolences compared to men. That's not good. I want to say that it is a theme that is interesting to examine more carefully and thoroughly. And then we've noticed that the new unit also finds that there are challenges associated with overseeing the two-day deadline. So what is referred to as the two-day deadline is the rule of the police code §3-1, which states that it should be transferred from police detention to regular prison within two days. It is stated in Section 3-1 of the Police Ordinance on the transfer from police detention to prison: ‘The prisoner shall be transferred within two days of the attack, unless this is not possible for practical reasons. If the prisoner is under 18 years of age, transfer shall be made as soon as possible and no later than the day following the attack. If a transfer is made later, the reasons shall be set out in the arrest record.’;» Over several years, the Civil Ombudsman has expressed concerns about the number of exceedances of the deadline. That's why we're worried about the fact that too long arrests in police arrests seemed to be an increasing problem in several places, including the United States. due to a lack of available capacity in prison. A joint committee says that they are happy that the prevention unit is aware of the problem of a two-day break. A single committee also points to the need to build capacity in prison as one of the key measures to address the problems. Finally, I would say that I believe I am talking to a joint committee when I say that we are pleased that this pre-emptive unit has now been created, and that one has done a good job. We thank you for a thorough first annual report for the unit.
